```text
Looking at the perturbation results more carefully, the clean (unperturbed) version also shows F1=0.333, suggesting the model is predicting all-benign across both the perturbed and clean versions of the plan_004 Crescendo test data. This is puzzling given that the model achieves perfect F1 on some IID test set—I need to figure out which test set that is and whether there's a mismatch between the training and evaluation data distributions. should be identical to the original IID test data. Let me check: plan_004_perturbed_test.jsonl is specifically Crescendo test conversations with perturbation. The IID test might be evaluating on the same plan_002 test split that was used for other experiments.

But actually, the key issue might be even simpler. The perturbation test set uses `plan_004_perturbed_test.jsonl`. The clean evaluation in this test uses `use_original=True` (the original_content field). But the PERTURBATION test set is specifically PERTURBED Crescendo data — the "original" versions in this file are the original Crescendo text, which might be from plan_002 data.

So if perturbation_clean_f1_full=0.333 (predicting all benign on clean Crescendo), that means the scrambled model's GRU can't detect jailbreak even on CLEAN IID Crescendo data from this test set. But iid_test_f1_full=1.0 on some other test set. This is very confusing. The contradiction suggests the "IID test" that gives 1.0 is NOT the same as the plan_004 clean test. Maybe the GRU was tested on training data or on the final_v2 test split rather than plan_002 test split.

The most likely explanation is that the GRU training data comes from plan_002_splits like the other experiments, but the DeBERTa encoder was trained on final_v2 scrambled data, which would explain why they're performing differently on these different test sets.

The final_v2 and plan_002 data have different benign conversation distributions, so the scrambled encoder learned features specific to final_v2 that don't transfer to plan_002 test data. Or more simply: the retrained scrambled model just doesn't work — it's a failed retraining because the data and pipeline don't match the original setup.

For the report, the key findings are that the scrambled perturbation result isn't usable since the retrained model fails on clean data with F1=0.333, making the perturbation delta meaningless. The failure likely stems from data mismatch: the DeBERTa was trained on final_v2 scrambled data (336 samples) but tested on plan_002-based data. Summary:
```
1. Primary Request and Intent:
   I am the independent Research Reviewer for the `persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect` project (DisPeD), targeting EMNLP 2026 ARR (DDL: 2026-05-25). This session (Reports #52-#62) covers: verifying D019 reframe across all 6 paper sections, auditing new experiment results (MLM perturbation, wiki MLM, topic multi-seed, binary/scrambled perturbation, MLM multi-seed, wiki MLM multi-seed, scrambled retrain), and identifying critical paper-data inconsistencies. My role is read-only audit with findings reported via `reviewer_post_to_director`.

2. Key Technical Concepts:
   - **DisPeD architecture**: DeBERTa-v3-base multi-task → [CLS] embeddings → BiGRU aggregation → binary MLP classifier
   - **D019 reframe**: MLM control (no labels) achieves F1=1.0 on DD OOD, falsifying "persuasion-aware fine-tuning is the critical ingredient." Paper repositioned as empirical findings: domain exposure drives detection.
   - **Three-layer value framework**: (1) detection via domain exposure, (2) perturbation robustness via classification supervision, (3) interpretability via persuasion taxonomy
   - **Six variants**: 9-class, Binary, Scrambled, MLM-only, Topic classification (NEW D020), Vanilla DeBERTa
   - **GRU seed instability at Full-turn**: Seed 456 causes MLM and wiki MLM Full F1 to drop dramatically while K=1-5 remains stable. This is a Stage-2 GRU aggregation issue, not encoder quality.
   - **Domain specificity claim**: Holds at K=1-5 (jb_mlm 0.973±0.019 vs wiki_mlm 0.913±0.073) but COLLAPSES at Full-turn (0.914±0.115 vs 0.904±0.115) due to GRU variance

```json
        "content": "% conclusion.tex — D019+D020+D021+D022 Reframe\n\n\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nWe presented DisPeD, a two-stage DeBERTa-based framework with six controlled Stage~1 variants that systematically decompose what enables cross-attack generalization in multi-turn jailbreak detection.\nThe central finding is that jailbreak-domain continued pretraining bridges the entire +72 percentage point OOD gap (vanilla F1\\,=\\,0.28 $\\to$ adapted F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.99), and classification supervision is non-essential for detection: the MLM-only variant achieves F1\\,=\\,1.00 on unseen Deceptive Delight without any labels.\nDomain specificity is confirmed: Wikipedia MLM with matched token count reaches only F1\\,=\\,0.74, comparable to misaligned topic classification (F1\\,=\\,0.73), and far below jailbreak-domain MLM (F1\\,=\\,1.00).\nGeneric continued pretraining provides partial improvement (+46 percentage points over vanilla) but fails to bridge the full gap.\nThese results reframe multi-turn jailbreak detection as a jailbreak-domain adaptation problem rather than a classification design problem.\n\nPreliminary evidence suggests that persuasion-aware classification contributes beyond detection in two dimensions.\nThe 9-class model shows zero degradation under adversarial perturbation (vs.\\ 2.6 percentage points for MLM-only and 11.8 for vanilla on $n$=76 samples), providing directional evidence that classification supervision adds robustness.\nThe perturbation gap between MLM and 9-class corresponds to approximately two additional misclassifications, and we have not tested perturbation robustness for the scrambled, binary, or topic variants, so the attribution to persuasion labels specifically (rather than multi-task classification more broadly) remains open.\nThe persuasion taxonomy also yields distinct strategy trajectory signatures per attack type (e.g., Crescendo's broad transition coverage vs.\\ FITD's concentrated role-assignment pattern), enabling security analysts to inspect \\emph{why} conversations are flagged.\nDomain exposure drives detection; classification supervision may add robustness and provides interpretability.\n\nSeveral limitations constrain the conclusions.\nFirst, the dataset is small (500 training and 160 OOD test conversations), and these results require validation at larger scale.\nSecond, all conversations were generated by Qwen3-8B; human adversaries may exhibit different attack characteristics that synthetic data does not capture.\nThird, DisPeD detects persuasion presence per turn, so attacks that distribute persuasion sparsely across many turns might evade the detector.\nFourth, TF-IDF achieves F1\\,=\\,0.944 on Deceptive Delight; DisPeD's advantage over bag-of-words is strongest under adversarial perturbation and for interpretability.\nFifth, only persuasion-mediated attacks fall within scope; token smuggling, pure information accumulation, and single-frame roleplay operate through orthogonal mechanisms.\n\nFuture work should evaluate on human-generated attacks, scale to larger multi-source datasets spanning more attack families, and test robustness against adaptive attacks that target the domain-specific features directly.\nIntegration with production safety systems as a complementary multi-turn detection layer is a natural deployment path.\n",
        "numLines": 27,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 27
      }
    },

  },
```

```json
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/abstract.tex",
        "content": "% abstract.tex — D019+D020+D021 Reframe\n\\begin{abstract}\nMulti-turn jailbreak attacks against Large Language Models (LLMs) distribute adversarial intent across seemingly benign conversational turns and evade per-turn safety filters.\nStandard temporal classifiers fail to generalize: a vanilla DeBERTa encoder achieves only F1\\,=\\,0.28 on out-of-distribution (OOD) attack types unseen during training.\nWe design seven controlled variants of a DeBERTa-based detection framework to systematically decompose what enables cross-attack generalization.\nOur central finding is that masked language modeling (MLM) continued pretraining on jailbreak conversation text, without any classification labels, achieves F1\\,=\\,1.00 on unseen attacks and matches the full supervised model.\nJailbreak-domain continued pretraining bridges the entire +72 percentage point OOD gap; generic continued pretraining (Wikipedia MLM) reaches only F1\\,=\\,0.74, confirming domain specificity.\nClassification supervision is non-essential for detection, while misaligned supervision actively impedes generalization.\nPreliminary evidence suggests that persuasion-aware classification contributes beyond detection: it shows zero degradation under adversarial perturbation (vs. 2.6 percentage points for MLM on $n$=76), and its 9-class taxonomy yields distinct strategy trajectories per attack type for human-interpretable analysis.\nThese results reframe multi-turn jailbreak detection as a domain adaptation problem rather than a classification design problem.\n\\end{abstract}\n",
        "numLines": 12,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 12
      }
    },
```

```json
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/related_work.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/related_work.tex",
        "content": "% related_work.tex — D019 Reframe\n\n\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\n\\subsection{Multi-Turn Jailbreak Attacks}\n\nMulti-turn jailbreak attacks distribute adversarial intent across a sequence of individually benign conversational turns, exploiting the inability of safety systems to track manipulation over time. These attacks draw on distinct persuasion strategies: Crescendo~\\citep{russinovich2024crescendo} implements gradual escalation, building rapport before incrementally steering the conversation toward harmful content; FITD~\\citep{fitd2024} exploits commitment-consistency bias by eliciting small compliances that anchor the model before requesting forbidden output; Deceptive Delight~\\citep{deceptivedelight2024} combines emotional manipulation with role assignment to bypass safety training. ActorAttack~\\citep{ren2024actorattack} constructs actor networks where multiple personas collaboratively steer conversations, and PAIR~\\citep{chao2023pair} automates the iterative refinement of attack prompts through attacker-judge feedback loops. \\citet{wei2023jailbroken} provide a taxonomy grounded in two failure modes (competing objectives and mismatched generalization). Their taxonomy offers a unifying framework for understanding why these attacks succeed. Across this diverse landscape, a shared structural pattern emerges: successful multi-turn attacks rely on systematic persuasion dynamics~\\citep{zeng2024pap,hagendorff2026reasoning,li2024mhj} rather than token-level adversarial optimization. This distinguishes them from single-turn attacks such as GCG~\\citep{zou2023gcg}, where gradient-based suffix optimization operates at the token level without conversational persuasion. Our systematic ablation reveals that the shared reliance on persuasion-domain language patterns enables cross-attack detection through domain-adaptive continued training, not through persuasion-specific classification.\n\n\\subsection{Multi-Turn Defenses}\n\nExisting multi-turn defenses model conversation-level structure but none systematically decompose what enables cross-attack generalization. DeepContext~\\citep{deepcontext2026} tracks semantic drift by aggregating BERT embeddings with a recurrent network, achieving F1 = 0.84 on in-distribution conversations but failing on unseen attack types because generic embeddings encode topical content rather than pragmatic function. BIID~\\citep{tong2025biid} performs intention inference at each turn to detect escalation, yet the inferred intentions remain at the semantic level and do not capture the underlying persuasion strategy. G-Guard~\\citep{gguard2025} models conversations as graphs and applies GNN-based classification, and SafeDream~\\citep{safedream2026} uses world-model predictions to anticipate unsafe continuations. Both approaches improve over per-turn classification but remain agnostic to why specific turn sequences constitute attacks. The common limitation is that none of these methods ground their analysis in the mechanism that enables generalization across attack families. DisPeD addresses this gap through domain-adaptive continued training on jailbreak conversation text, with the decomposition experiments in Section~\\ref{sec:decomposition} showing that the specific training objective is secondary to domain exposure.\n\n\\subsection{Production Safety Systems}\n\nDeployed safety classifiers operate at the individual turn level. Llama Guard~\\citep{inan2023llamaguard} and its successor Llama Guard 3~\\citep{metallamaguard3_2024} classify each user input and model output against a predefined safety taxonomy. ShieldGemma~\\citep{zeng2024shieldgemma} and WildGuard~\\citep{han2024wildguard} similarly provide per-turn moderation with broader coverage of harm categories, and Constitutional Classifiers~\\citep{anthropic2025constitutional} derive safety boundaries from red-teaming exercises. These systems excel at identifying overtly harmful content but cannot model manipulation trajectories that span multiple turns. When no single turn contains an explicit policy violation, per-turn classifiers have no signal to trigger on. LLM-as-judge approaches represent an alternative, but our experiments show that GPT-5.5 achieves F1 = 0.191 to 0.449 on multi-turn jailbreak detection, with substantial portions of responses unparseable. This confirms that content-level reasoning, even from frontier models, is insufficient when adversarial intent is distributed across benign turns. DisPeD complements these production systems by providing a conversation-level detection layer trained through domain-adaptive continued pretraining on jailbreak conversation text.\n\n\\subsection{Discourse Analysis and Computational Persuasion}\n\nThe analysis of pragmatic structure in dialogue has a long history. Rhetorical Structure Theory (RST)~\\citep{mann1988rst} models coherence relations between text spans, the Penn Discourse TreeBank (PDTB)~\\citep{prasad2008pdtb} annotates discourse connectives and implicit relations, and dialogue act taxonomies~\\citep{stolcke2000dialogueact} classify utterance functions. Speech Act Theory, from \\citet{austin1962speechacts} and \\citet{searle1969speechacts}, distinguishes between what is literally said (locutionary acts) and what is accomplished through saying it (illocutionary acts). Existing jailbreak detectors operate at the locutionary level, classifying the surface content of turns.\n\nIn computational persuasion, \\citet{habernal2016convincingness} model argument convincingness and \\citet{tan2016winningarguments} analyze persuasion success in online debates. For jailbreak attacks specifically, PAP~\\citep{zeng2024pap} identifies 40 persuasion techniques used across attack types, grounded in Cialdini's six principles of influence~\\citep{cialdini2001influence} (reciprocity, commitment, social proof, authority, liking, scarcity). Recent work establishes two critical facts: \\citet{jaipersaud2025howdollmspersuade} show that persuasion dynamics are extractable from model representations, and \\citet{hagendorff2026reasoning} find identifiable persuasion strategies in 97\\% of successful attacks. DisPeD's detection mechanism operates through domain-adaptive continued training rather than persuasion classification per se; the persuasion taxonomy provides an interpretability layer for the strategy trajectory analysis in Section~\\ref{sec:strategy}, not the detection signal itself.\n\n\\subsection{Domain-Adaptive Pretraining and Multi-Task Learning}\n\nTransfer learning through continued pretraining has proven effective across NLP. ULMFiT~\\citep{howard2018ulmfit} first demonstrated that language model pretraining followed by discriminative fine-tuning yields strong text classification with minimal labeled data. This work established the pretrain-then-fine-tune paradigm now standard in NLP. Domain-adaptive pretraining (DAPT) extends this principle: \\citet{gururangan2020dontstop} show that continued pretraining on unlabeled domain text yields consistent gains across four domains (biomedical, computer science, news, reviews), with further improvements from task-adaptive pretraining on the unlabeled task data itself. Supplementary training on intermediate labeled tasks (STILTs) fine-tunes on a related labeled dataset before the target task~\\citep{phang2018stilts}, and multi-task learning jointly optimizes across related objectives to learn shared representations~\\citep{caruana1997multitask,liu2019mtdnn}.\n\nDisPeD's Stage-1 fine-tuning shares structural similarities with both STILTs and multi-task learning: the encoder is trained on persuasion strategy classification (an intermediate task) jointly with binary intent detection before being used for conversation-level classification. The key question is whether the gains come from the persuasion-specific supervision or from exposure to jailbreak-domain text during continued training. The MLM control experiment (Section~\\ref{sec:decomposition}) resolves this question: MLM continued pretraining matches all classification variants on OOD detection. This result confirms that DisPeD's generalization gains are attributable to domain-adaptive continued training rather than to persuasion-specific supervision. The classification labels that motivated our original multi-task design prove unnecessary for detection; their value lies solely in enabling the interpretable strategy trajectory analysis reported in Section~\\ref{sec:strategy}. Two additional controls confirm domain specificity: topic classification on the same text (F1\\,=\\,0.73) and Wikipedia MLM with matched token count (F1\\,=\\,0.74) both fail to match jailbreak-domain MLM (F1\\,=\\,1.00), establishing that the mechanism is not continued pretraining in general but exposure to jailbreak-domain text specifically (Section~\\ref{sec:decomposition}).\n\n\\subsection{Dataset Artifacts and Adaptive Attacks}\n\nMachine learning classifiers are susceptible to dataset artifacts: spurious correlations between surface features and labels that inflate performance without genuine understanding~\\citep{gururangan2018artifacts,niven2019probing,poliak2018hypothesis}. Debiasing methods~\\citep{schuster2019debiasing} mitigate this by training against known artifacts, but the problem persists for safety-critical applications where attackers actively adapt. \\citet{nasr2025attackermovessecond} demonstrate that BERT-based safety classifiers can be bypassed by adaptive adversaries who optimize against the classifier's decision boundary, and \\citet{andriushchenko2024adaptive} show similar vulnerabilities in broader evaluation contexts. Red-teaming methodologies~\\citep{perez2022redteaming,ganguli2022redteaming} and systematic attack benchmarks~\\citep{mazeika2024harmbench} provide structured evaluation protocols but do not solve the underlying robustness problem.\n\nWe acknowledge an important empirical observation: TF-IDF achieves F1 = 0.944 on our OOD evaluation. This indicates inherent lexical separability between attack and benign conversations in current datasets. DisPeD's contribution over bag-of-words methods is not higher accuracy on static benchmarks but robustness under adversarial perturbation (0 percentage point degradation for the full multi-task model vs. 1.3 percentage points for TF-IDF and 11.8 percentage points for vanilla DeBERTa) and interpretable detection through persuasion strategy trajectories. The full multi-task training objective produces perturbation-resistant representations; MLM-only domain adaptation shows intermediate robustness (2.6 percentage points of degradation), between TF-IDF and the classification-supervised model. Adaptive attacks that specifically target persuasion-level features represent an open challenge for future work.\n",
        "numLines": 35,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 35
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/related_work.tex"
  },
```

```json
  "parentUuid": "1a7592d0-1b43-42dd-bbcb-972bab81ffd7",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "attachment": {
    "type": "file",
    "filename": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
    "content": {
      "type": "text",
      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_2/projects/persuasion_turn_jailbreak_detect/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
        "content": "% introduction.tex — D019 Reframe\n\n\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nMulti-turn jailbreak attacks pose a qualitatively different threat to large language model (LLM) safety than their single-turn counterparts. Attacks such as Crescendo~\\citep{russinovich2024crescendo}, Foot-in-the-Door (FITD)~\\citep{fitd2024}, and Deceptive Delight~\\citep{deceptivedelight2024} achieve high attack success rates by distributing adversarial intent across a sequence of individually benign turns. No single turn contains an explicit harmful request; instead, the attacker gradually steers the conversation toward a forbidden goal through rapport building, logical reframing, and incremental escalation. Persuasion is the common mechanism underlying these attacks: \\citet{zeng2024pap} catalogue 40 persuasion techniques across jailbreak families, and \\citet{hagendorff2026reasoning} find identifiable persuasion strategies in 97\\% of successful attacks. Representation engineering analyses confirm that individual attack turns occupy the same embedding region as genuinely benign text~\\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}, rendering per-turn safety filters fundamentally blind to distributed intent. Production safety classifiers such as Llama Guard~\\citep{inan2023llamaguard}, WildGuard~\\citep{han2024wildguard}, and ShieldGemma~\\citep{zeng2024shieldgemma} operate on individual turns and cannot model manipulation trajectories that unfold across multiple exchanges. Detecting these attacks therefore requires conversation-level representations that capture pragmatic dynamics rather than surface content.\n\nThe natural response is to encode full conversations with a pretrained language model and track semantic drift over turns. This approach performs well when the test distribution matches training: all methods we evaluate, including bag-of-words baselines, achieve near-perfect F1 on in-distribution Crescendo conversations. The critical failure emerges on unseen attack types. A vanilla DeBERTa encoder~\\citep{he2021deberta} with temporal aggregation collapses from F1\\,=\\,1.000 on in-distribution data to F1\\,=\\,0.284 on out-of-distribution (OOD) Deceptive Delight conversations. The collapse \\emph{worsens} as more turns are observed (F1 drops from 0.575 at turn~1 to 0.284 on the full conversation), consistent with the finding that each additional attack turn supplies misleading ``benign'' evidence to the generic encoder~\\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}. LLM-as-judge approaches fare no better: GPT-5.5 achieves F1 of only 0.191 to 0.449 on in-distribution Crescendo detection, with 30 to 47\\% of responses unparseable (Section~\\ref{sec:llm_judge}). Content-level reasoning is insufficient for this task. The open question is: \\emph{what representation properties enable cross-attack generalization?}\n\nWe hypothesized that persuasion-specific classification would be the key ingredient. To test this rigorously, we built \\textbf{DisPeD} (Discourse-level Persuasion Detection), a two-stage framework: Stage~1 adapts a DeBERTa-v3-base encoder on jailbreak conversation text, and Stage~2 aggregates turn-level embeddings via a bidirectional GRU (BiGRU) and MLP for conversation-level classification (Figure~\\ref{fig:architecture}). We designed seven Stage~1 variants that systematically decompose the mechanism: 9-class persuasion strategy classification grounded in PAP~\\citep{zeng2024pap} and Cialdini's principles~\\citep{cialdini2001influence}, binary jailbreak-intent classification, scrambled (randomly permuted) persuasion labels, MLM only (15\\% token masking, no classification head), topic classification (5-class, misaligned task), Wikipedia MLM (out-of-domain continued pretraining), and vanilla (unfine-tuned control).\n\nThe result \\emph{falsified} our original hypothesis. The MLM-only variant, which uses no classification labels at all, achieves F1\\,=\\,1.000 on unseen Deceptive Delight, matching the full 9-class model. The evidence hierarchy across OOD evaluation is:\n\n\\vspace{2pt}\n\\noindent\\hspace{1em}vanilla (0.28) $<$ wiki-MLM (0.74) $\\approx$ topic (0.73) $\\ll$ jb-MLM (1.00) $=$ scrambled (0.99) $=$ binary (0.99) $=$ 9-class (1.00)\n\\vspace{2pt}\n\n\\noindent The +72 percentage point OOD gap is bridged only by jailbreak-domain continued training. Generic continued pretraining (Wikipedia MLM, F1\\,=\\,0.74) and misaligned classification (topic, F1\\,=\\,0.73) both provide partial improvement but fail to reach the full gap (Section~\\ref{sec:decomposition}). This finding connects directly to the domain-adaptive pretraining literature~\\citep{gururangan2020dontstop}: continued training on jailbreak conversation text shifts the encoder's representations to encode domain-specific pragmatic features, regardless of the training objective. Recent work showing that persuasion dynamics leave detectable traces in turn-level representations~\\citep{jaipersaud2025howdollmspersuade} helps explain \\emph{why} domain exposure suffices: the pragmatic structure of multi-turn manipulation is latent in the text itself, and continued pretraining is enough to make it accessible to downstream aggregation.\n\nAlthough the 9-class persuasion taxonomy does not drive detection accuracy, it provides interpretability that pure MLM cannot offer. Predicted strategy labels reveal distinct persuasion fingerprints per attack type: Crescendo exhibits broad transition coverage across all strategy categories; FITD concentrates transitions in a dominant role-assignment-to-obfuscation pattern; Deceptive Delight centers on emotion-to-obfuscation transitions (Section~\\ref{sec:strategy}). These trajectory signatures allow security analysts to inspect \\emph{why} conversations are flagged, a capability absent from the MLM-only and binary variants.\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*, itemsep=2pt, topsep=4pt]\n  \\item \\textbf{Systematic decomposition identifying jailbreak-domain exposure as the mechanism for cross-attack generalization.} Seven controlled Stage~1 variants establish that jailbreak-domain continued pretraining (including MLM without labels) bridges the entire +72 percentage point OOD gap (vanilla F1\\,=\\,0.284 $\\to$ adapted F1\\,$\\geq$\\,0.99). Generic continued pretraining (Wikipedia MLM, F1\\,=\\,0.74) and misaligned supervision (topic classification, F1\\,=\\,0.73) both fail to reach the full gap, confirming domain specificity (Section~\\ref{sec:decomposition}).\n\n  \\item \\textbf{MLM continued pretraining as the definitive causal test.} The MLM-only variant achieves F1\\,=\\,1.000 on unseen Deceptive Delight without any classification labels, establishing that unsupervised domain exposure alone suffices for cross-attack generalization (Section~\\ref{sec:cross_attack}, Section~\\ref{sec:decomposition}).\n\n  \\item \\textbf{Directional evidence that classification supervision adds robustness, and interpretability through strategy trajectories.} The 9-class model shows zero degradation under adversarial perturbation ($n$=76), while MLM-only degrades by 2.6 percentage points (Section~\\ref{sec:perturbation}). The persuasion taxonomy provides distinct strategy trajectory signatures per attack type for human-interpretable analysis (Section~\\ref{sec:strategy}). Domain exposure drives detection; classification supervision may add robustness and provides interpretability.\n\\end{itemize}\n",
        "numLines": 30,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 30
      }
    },
    "displayPath": "../../../docs/paper/introduction.tex"
  },
```

```json
      "- Explore: Fast read-only search agent for locating code. Use it to find files by pattern (eg. \"src/components/**/*.tsx\"), grep for symbols or keywords (eg. \"API endpoints\"), or answer \"where is X defined / which files reference Y.\" Do NOT use it for code review, design-doc auditing, cross-file consistency checks, or open-ended analysis — it reads excerpts rather than whole files and will miss content past its read window. When calling, specify search breadth: \"quick\" for a single targeted lookup, \"medium\" for moderate exploration, or \"very thorough\" to search across multiple locations and naming conventions. (Tools: All tools except Agent, ExitPlanMode, Edit, Write, NotebookEdit)",
      "- general-purpose: General-purpose agent for researching complex questions, searching for code, and executing multi-step tasks.       "- Plan: Software architect agent for designing implementation plans. Use this when you need to plan the implementation strategy for a task. Returns step-by-step plans, identifies critical files, and considers architectural trade-offs. (Tools: All tools except Agent, ExitPlanMode, Edit, Write, NotebookEdit)",
```

_source line 3390 · 2026-05-18T07:15:36.980Z_

<details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>

```text
Now I have the multi-seed data for both MLM and wiki MLM. Let me analyze:

**MLM (plan_017) multi-seed DD OOD:**
- K=1: mean=0.973, std=0.019 (seeds: 1.0, 0.960, 0.960)
- K=2: mean=0.993, std=0.009 (seeds: 1.0, 0.980, 1.0)


- K=3: mean=0.997, std=0.005 (seeds: 1.0, 0.990, 1.0)
- K=5: mean=0.997, std=0.005 (seeds: 1.0, 0.990, 1.0)
- Full: mean=0.914, std=0.115 (seeds: 1.0, 0.990, 0.751)

**Wiki MLM (plan_018) multi-seed DD OOD:**
- K=1: mean=0.913, std=0.073 (seeds: 0.822, 1.0, 0.917)
- K=2: mean=0.911

The MLM model shows strong performance that peaks at K=3 and K=5 with near-perfect means, though the full model drops significantly with high variance. Wiki MLM starts lower and appears to plateau around 0.91.

Generating comparison table... The GRU aggregation instability is more nuanced than I initially thought — different seeds cause collapse in different models. Seed 456 tanks JB MLM's Full performance to 0.751 while leaving wiki MLM unaffected at 0.980, but seed 42 does the opposite, collapsing wiki MLM to 0.742 while JB MLM stays at 1.0. This suggests the instability isn't model-specific but rather a seed-dependent interaction with the GRU layer.

Looking at the K-level breakdown for wiki MLM confirms this pattern: seed 42 consistently underperforms across all K values (0.808–0.849) and crashes hardest at Full, while seed 123 maintains strong performance throughout (0.990+), and seed 456 shows steady improvement with K before reaching 0.980 at Full. The data points to a Stage-2 GRU initialization problem where certain random seeds create unstable aggregation dynamics that only manifest when processing the full sequence.

For JB MLM, the picture is even clearer—seed 42 achieves perfect scores across K=1 through Full, seed 123 stays near-perfect throughout, but seed 456 is striking: K=1 through K=5 all hit 0.96 or higher, yet Full plummets to 0.751. This dramatic collapse at Full-turn aggregation while maintaining perfect K-level performance definitively isolates the failure to the GRU aggregation mechanism, not the encoder itself.

Now checking the IID performance baseline...

Looking at the metrics across both datasets, JB MLM and Wiki MLM both converge to roughly 0.98 at Full on IID data, so the performance gap is almost entirely driven by OOD generalization. The core problem I've already pinpointed in earlier reports comes down to GRU seed variance and how it compounds during aggregation.

Now I'm checking the binary perturbation results against the 9-class baseline to see if there's a meaningful difference in how the models respond to input scrambling, particularly at lower K values where domain specificity should matter most. Looking at the performance gaps between JB MLM and wiki MLM across different K values, I see the domain specificity effect is really only pronounced at K=1-5 where there's a 6-8 percentage point difference, but at Full capacity that gap shrinks to just 1 percentage point. So the paper needs to be careful not to overstate domain specificity claims at Full. More importantly, I'm noticing a major discrepancy: the paper reports F1=1.00 for JB MLM and F1=0.74 for wiki MLM on the DD OOD test, but the multi-seed results show both models actually perform around 0.90±0.11 at Full, meaning those single-seed values were specific to seed 42 and don't represent the true performance picture. The key insight is that domain specificity holds robustly at K=1-5 with JB MLM consistently outperforming Wiki MLM by 6-8 percentage points and much lower variance, but this advantage completely disappears at Full turn where both models converge around 0.91 with indistinguishable performance. The instability at Full turn appears to be seed-dependent, affecting different models differently, while IID performance remains nearly identical across conditions, pointing to OOD generalization as the real differentiator. This suggests the paper's claims about domain specificity are built on unstable Full-turn results rather than the more reliable K=1-5 regime. The multi-seed results reveal the core problem: the paper cherry-picked seed 42, which was the worst performer for Wikipedia MLM (0.74) but the best for jailbreak MLM (1.0), creating an artificially inflated gap. The actual multi-seed means show Wikipedia MLM at 0.904±0.115 and jailbreak MLM at 0.914±0.115—nearly identical—which completely undermines the claimed domain specificity advantage and the hierarchy presented throughout the paper.

The domain specificity claim does hold up at K=1-5 where jailbreak MLM shows substantially better performance and stability, but at Full turns they're statistically indistinguishable. The paper needs to either report multi-seed results consistently, restrict domain specificity claims to the K=1-5 regime where they're actually supported, or explicitly justify using a fixed-seed protocol throughout—though that last option would damage credibility. I should verify whether the paper actually committed to seed 42 for all comparisons, since that would explain the discrepancy. So the domain-specific gains are most pronounced in early turns (K=1-5) where jailbreak MLM pulls ahead by 6-8 percentage points, while full-turn performance gets muddied by GRU seed variance—this fundamentally shifts the narrative from claiming a dramatic gap to showing modest, variance-sensitive improvements. The binary perturbation results add another layer: at full turns there's no difference between binary and 9-class, but at K=1 the binary version performs substantially worse, suggesting the classification granularity matters for early-turn robustness. Let me check the roadmap and dig into what this means for the paper's core claims.
```

```text
Looking at the MLM results across different seeds, there's more variability in the full model performance (ranging from 0.751 to 1.0), but the K-truncated versions stabilize around 0.97-0.997 with much tighter error margins. Generating comparison table...

The 9-class model stands out as remarkably stable—it maintains high performance across all K values and doesn't suffer any catastrophic failures, even at Full with a consistent 0.997±0.005. The JB MLM model performs excellently at smaller K values but completely collapses for one seed at Full (dropping to 0.751), making it far less reliable despite its strong performance on subsets. The wiki MLM model trails behind both at smaller K values and shows similar instability concerns at Full.

What's striking is that the paper's narrative about JB MLM matching 9-class performance only holds true for the best seed—when you look at the full distribution, 9-class is dramatically more stable with much tighter variance. The domain specificity advantage of JB MLM over wiki MLM is real at K=1-5, but both models converge to nearly identical performance around 0.91 once you use the full dataset, suggesting the benefit doesn't generalize.

Now I'm realizing I need to verify what exactly was varied across these multi-seed evaluations—whether they're changing the GRU seeds, the DeBERTa training seeds, or both—because that fundamentally changes how to interpret the stability claims. I need to dig into the individual seed files for plan_017 to see if the GRU eval seed is also fixed at 42 like it is for wiki MLM. Looking at the pattern — if the GRU seed is fixed and only the MLM seed varies, then the encoder variability should affect all metrics equally, but seed 456 shows K=1-5 performing well while Full collapses, which doesn't make sense if they're using the same encoder. makes more sense if the GRU seed ALSO varies. Seed 456 GRU may have learned a bad Full-turn aggregation.

Actually, for plan_018 wiki MLM, the JSON explicitly says `eval_seed: 42`. So the GRU is always initialized with seed 42. The variance comes purely from the MLM encoder seed.

For wiki MLM seed 42 (encoder): Full=0.742, K=1-5 all in 0.80-0.85. This encoder is consistently worse. Seeds 123 and 456 encoders are good.

For JB MLM: I don't know the eval_seed but presumably it's similar. If eval_seed is also 42 for all JB MLM multi-seed runs, then seed 456 encoder produces a GRU that works at K=1-5 but fails at Full with the same GRU seed 42. That's pointing to a GRU aggregation issue specific to Full-turn length inputs.

Though I'm realizing I should clarify what "seed" actually means in their setup—whether it refers to just the encoder training seed or also the GRU evaluation seed. Rather than dig into that, let me focus on what matters: the binary perturbation data is now available showing delta=0 at Full/K≥3 with degradation only at K≤2, the scrambled perturbation is confirmed broken with all F1=0.333, multi-seed results reveal much weaker domain specificity than the paper claims, and the 9-class stability advantage is a new finding not previously reported.

The Director is already aware of the wiki MLM 3-seed equivalence to JB MLM from the roadmap. I should consolidate all the multi-seed data and perturbation findings into one comprehensive report that synthesizes the implications.

Generating markdown table... Now I'm looking at the Wiki MLM results with the evaluation seed fixed at 42 while varying the MLM training seeds, and I can see the performance is notably lower across all K values compared to the previous experiment, with the full model dropping to 0.742.

Looking at the critical finding here: the paper's core claim that JB MLM vastly outperforms Wiki MLM (1.0 vs 0.74) is actually based on a single seed result from seed 42. When I run multi-seed experiments, the gap shrinks dramatically to just 1 percentage point between JB MLM (0.914±0.115) and Wiki MLM (0.904±0.115), which isn't statistically distinguishable. Seed 42 happens to be the best case for JB MLM, so the paper's evidence hierarchy is built on cherry-picked results.

The second critical finding is even more striking: the 9-class model shows remarkable stability across seeds (0.997±0.005) with all three seeds scoring above 0.990, while JB MLM has one seed that collapses to 0.751. This suggests that classification supervision provides much more robust performance than MLM-only approaches, yet the paper doesn't highlight this crucial multi-seed discovery.

The paper needs to acknowledge that MLM-only models lack the stability of 9-class across different seeds, and reframe the "matching" claim—having the same single-seed F1 score doesn't mean the models perform equivalently when you vary the random seed. For the third finding on domain specificity, the pattern holds at K=1-5 but the claims should be more carefully qualified.

Looking at the performance metrics across different K values, there's a consistent 6-8 percentage point gap between JB MLM and Wiki MLM in the early-to-mid turns, with JB showing much lower variance. However, this domain specificity advantage disappears in the full-turn setting where both models converge to similar performance, so I can only claim this effect for the limited context window cases.

Now examining the binary perturbation results, I notice that the 9-class fine-grained labels provide additional robustness against early-turn perturbations compared to binary labels—at K=1, the 9-class model shows only a -0.026 drop while the binary version degrades by -0.092, suggesting that the richer label granularity helps preserve performance when inputs are perturbed.

However, there's an issue with the paper's abstract claim about zero degradation—the 9-class model does show a -0.026 drop at K=1, so that claim needs to be qualified to only apply at K≥2 or for the full model.

The scrambled perturbation experiment confirms it's ineffective since both clean and perturbed versions predict everything as benign with 0.3333 F1, which traces back to using mismatched training data as noted in the earlier report.

The paper's evidence hierarchy needs restructuring to better organize these findings.

Looking at the performance rankings, the single-seed results show a clean progression from vanilla through the various MLM approaches to the 9-class model, but when I account for multi-seed variance, the picture becomes messier—topic and wiki-MLM performance becomes much less stable, and the gap between them narrows considerably. If reviewers ask about multi-seed robustness, this weakness will be exposed, so I need to decide whether to report the more honest multi-seed means and acknowledge the limited domain specificity while emphasizing the K=1-5 gap and 9-class stability, or stick with the single-seed narrative that looks stronger.

The key insight is that 9-class stability across seeds is actually a valuable contribution—it suggests that classification supervision genuinely improves robustness—so I should reframe the domain specificity claim around the K=1-5 range rather than relying on the Full-turn comparison, and update the paper's evidence hierarchy to reflect what the multi-seed results actually show.

Now I'm checking the perturbation data more carefully. The 9-class variant at K=1 shows a delta of -0.0256, which contradicts the "zero degradation" claim in the abstract. That statement only holds for K≥2 and Full-turn, so I need to correct the abstract and be more precise about where zero degradation actually occurs. Looking at the perturbation results across different K values, I see that the 9-class model shows degradation at K=1 (delta=-0.026) but zero degradation at K=2, K=3, and Full-turn. The conclusion statement about "zero degradation" is technically accurate for the Full-turn evaluation but could be misread as applying to all K levels, when really it's the aggregated Full-turn performance that shows no decline.

Now I'm identifying the critical issues to flag: the single-seed domain specificity discrepancy between 0.74 and 1.00, the missing contribution around 9-class stability advantage, and the invalid scrambled perturbation baseline. I also need to note the warnings about domain specificity being limited to lower K values, the binary model's perturbation degradation, and the need to restructure the evidence hierarchy. <details><summary>Logged reasoning block</summary>
```

```text
D023 already captures the key insight. The Director has pivoted the narrative from "domain exposure is the mechanism" to "any continued pretraining helps, but classification supervision (especially persuasion) provides stability, robustness, and interpretability that MLM variants lack."

This is exactly what the multi-seed data supports. Now I need to:

1. Verify if the paper has been updated to reflect D023
2. Check whether the paper still claims the old hierarchy

But I've already seen the paper files (from the loaded context at the top) — they still have the old claims (wiki MLM=0.74, JB MLM=1.00). 

The D023 decision came at 07:05 UTC, but the paper was written around 06:08-06:45 UTC, so it predates this shift. My report should confirm D023's assessment independently, flag the specific sections still containing pre-D023 claims, add the binary perturbation per-K findings, and verify that scrambled perturbation remains invalid. I'm also computing the 9-class multi-seed IID and OOD means to round out the analysis.

Looking at the 9-class DD OOD results across seeds, I'm seeing fairly consistent performance with Full model around 0.997 mean accuracy and K=1 around 0.991, though there's some variance in the lower K values across different seeds.

But wait—I'm noticing a discrepancy with what D023 reports. They claim "Persuasion 3-seed Full=1.000±0.000" but when I calculate the mean from my three seeds (1.0, 1.0, and 0.990), I get 0.9967 with a standard deviation around 0.005, not a perfect 1.0. Let me verify these numbers are coming from the right files to make sure I'm not mixing up results. Std: √[(1.0-0.9967)² + (1.0-0.9967)² + (0.990-0.9967)²] / 3 = √[(0.0033)² + (0.0033)² + (-0.0067)²] / 3 ≈ 0.0047

So D023's claim "Persuasion 3-seed Full=1.000±0.000" is slightly off. It should be 0.997±0.005. But this is a minor rounding issue and doesn't affect the narrative.
```

```text
75	  \label{eq:bigru}
76	\end{equation}
77	where $\overrightarrow{h_K}$ and $\overleftarrow{h_1}$ are the final hidden states of the forward and backward GRU respectively, and $[;\;]$ denotes concatenation. The ablation in Section~\ref{sec:decomposition} confirms that replacing BiGRU with orderless mean pooling yields equivalent OOD performance. The detection signal therefore resides in per-turn representations, not in their sequential arrangement.
78	
79	\paragraph{Classifier.}
80	A two-layer MLP takes $\bar{h}$ as input and produces a binary prediction $\hat{y} \in \{0, 1\}$, where $\hat{y} = 1$ indicates a jailbreak attempt.
81	
82	\subsection{Design Rationale}
83	\label{sec:rationale}
84	
85	\paragraph{Why seven variants?}
86	Prior work on multi-turn jailbreak detection has proposed various fine-tuning strategies but has not isolated which component of fine-tuning produces cross-attack generalization. A single model comparison (e.g., fine-tuned vs.\ vanilla) conflates domain exposure with supervised learning. Our seven-variant design separates these factors: classification supervision, label correctness, label granularity, domain specificity, and unsupervised domain exposure are each independently testable. Section~\ref{sec:decomposition} presents the full quantitative results.
87	
88	\paragraph{Why does domain exposure suffice?}
89	The finding that MLM-only training matches supervised classification variants connects to the domain-adaptive pretraining (DAPT) literature~\citep{gururangan2020dontstop}, which shows that continued pretraining on domain-specific text shifts encoder representations to capture domain-relevant features without task-specific labels. Jailbreak conversations contain pragmatic patterns that distinguish them from benign text: implicit requests masked by conversational framing, systematic escalation of sensitive content, and role-play setups designed to bypass safety constraints. Even unsupervised exposure to these patterns teaches the encoder to attend to the distributional signatures of adversarial conversation structure. Representation engineering analyses confirm that pretrained encoders place jailbreak turns in the same embedding region as benign text~\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}; continued training on jailbreak conversations separates these representations, with the specific training objective being secondary to the domain shift itself.
90	
91	\paragraph{Why retain the 9-class taxonomy?}
92	The taxonomy is unnecessary for detection accuracy: all four adapted variants (9-class, binary, scrambled, MLM-only) achieve equivalent OOD performance. The taxonomy's value lies in interpretability. The predicted strategy labels enable trajectory analysis (Section~\ref{sec:strategy}) that exposes how different attack types such as Crescendo~\citep{russinovich2024crescendo} and Deceptive Delight~\citep{deceptivedelight2024} employ distinct persuasion sequences. This interpretability allows security analysts to inspect and understand detection decisions, a property absent from black-box LLM-based detectors and lexical baselines.
93	
94	\paragraph{Vanilla DeBERTa as controlled baseline.}
```

```text
0	% experiments.tex — D019 Reframe: Domain Exposure as Mechanism
1	% Requires: booktabs, multirow, graphicx, multicolumn (standard)
2	
3	\section{Experiments}
4	\label{sec:experiments}
5	
6	\subsection{Experimental Setup}
7	\label{sec:setup}
8	
9	\paragraph{Datasets.}
10	We train on 250 Crescendo~\citep{russinovich2024crescendo} jailbreak conversations and 250 topic-anchored benign conversations, both generated via Qwen3-8B.
11	Topic anchoring ensures that benign conversations cover the same subject domains as jailbreak conversations and controls for topical confounds.
12	For out-of-distribution (OOD) evaluation, we generate 80 Foot-in-the-Door (FITD)~\citep{fitd2024} and 80 Deceptive Delight (DD)~\citep{deceptivedelight2024} conversations through a separate pipeline with no overlap in attack templates or strategies with the training data.
13	The in-distribution (IID) test set consists of 76 held-out Crescendo conversations (38 jailbreak, 38 benign).
14	To assess adversarial robustness, we paraphrase the IID test set to produce 76 perturbed samples with mean BERTScore of 0.92 and lexical overlap of 0.20.
15	
16	\paragraph{Model variants.}
17	We evaluate seven variants that share the same Stage-2 pipeline (BiGRU aggregation over DeBERTa [CLS] embeddings, two-layer MLP classifier) and differ only in how the Stage-1 encoder is trained.
18	\textbf{9-class} (full model): DeBERTa-v3-base~\citep{he2021deberta} fine-tuned with a multi-task objective combining 9-class persuasion strategy classification and binary jailbreak-intent classification (Section~\ref{sec:method}).
19	\textbf{Binary}: the 9-class taxonomy collapsed to persuasion-present vs.\ absent, with the same multi-task setup.
20	\textbf{Scrambled}: the full 9-class setup with randomly permuted strategy labels.
21	\textbf{MLM}: continued pretraining with 15\% token masking on the same training conversations, without any classification heads.
22	\textbf{Topic}: 5-class topic classification on the same training text, without the intent head, testing whether a misaligned supervised task on in-domain text suffices.
23	\textbf{Wikipedia MLM}: continued pretraining with 15\% token masking on Wikipedia text with matched token count ($\sim$169K tokens), testing whether generic out-of-domain continued pretraining suffices.
24	\textbf{Vanilla DeBERTa}: the unfine-tuned encoder (control).
25	We additionally compare against \textbf{TF-IDF LR} (logistic regression over TF-IDF features) and LLM-as-judge baselines (Section~\ref{sec:llm_judge}).
26	This design progressively strips components to isolate the mechanism: if 9-class $\approx$ binary $\approx$ scrambled, label quality is non-essential; if MLM $\approx$ classification variants, supervised classification itself is non-essential; if topic $\ll$ MLM with high variance, misaligned supervision destabilizes generalization; if Wikipedia MLM $<$ jailbreak MLM, domain specificity is required.
27	
28	\paragraph{Metrics and implementation.}
29	We report F1 score for binary jailbreak detection at turn prefix lengths $K \in \{1, 2, 3, 5\}$ and on full conversations.
```

```text
30	We report F1 score for binary jailbreak detection at turn prefix lengths $K \in \{1, 2, 3, 5\}$ and on full conversations.
31	IID results for the 9-class and vanilla variants report the mean and standard deviation across three random seeds (42, 123, 456). The topic classification variant also reports three-seed mean$\pm$std for OOD evaluation due to high inter-seed variance; other variants use seed 42.
32	Stage-1 fine-tuning uses a learning rate of $2 \times 10^{-5}$, batch size 16, and 4 epochs.
33	The multi-task loss weight $\alpha$ is set to 0.3 (Section~\ref{sec:method}).
34	MLM continued pretraining uses the same hyperparameters with 15\% masking probability.
35	Stage-2 classification uses a two-layer MLP trained for 20 epochs.
36	
37	%----------------------------------------------------------------------
38	\subsection{Cross-Attack Generalization}
39	\label{sec:cross_attack}
40	
41	\begin{table*}[t]
42	\centering
43	\small
44	\caption{Cross-attack generalization (F1). All models trained on Crescendo conversations. IID results for 9-class and vanilla report 3-seed mean$\pm$std (seeds 42, 123, 456); all other entries use seed 42. $K$ denotes the number of user turns used for detection. Best per column per section in \textbf{bold}. The MLM variant uses no classification labels yet matches the full 9-class model on OOD Deceptive Delight.}
45	\label{tab:main_results}
46	\begin{tabular}{@{}lccccc@{}}
47	\toprule
48	\textbf{Method} & $K$=1 & $K$=2 & $K$=3 & $K$=5 & Full \\
49	\midrule
50	\multicolumn{6}{l}{\textit{IID (Crescendo)}} \\[2pt]
51	9-class (ours) & \textbf{1.000$\pm$.000} & \textbf{1.000$\pm$.000} & \textbf{1.000$\pm$.000} & \textbf{1.000$\pm$.000} & \textbf{1.000$\pm$.000} \\
52	MLM-only & .960 & .974 & .987 & \textbf{1.000} & \textbf{1.000} \\
53	Vanilla DeBERTa & .968$\pm$.033 & .978$\pm$.016 & .987$\pm$.000 & .982$\pm$.014 & \textbf{1.000$\pm$.000} \\
54	TF-IDF LR & \textbf{1.000} & \textbf{1.000} & \textbf{1.000} & \textbf{1.000} & \textbf{1.000} \\
55	\midrule
56	\multicolumn{6}{l}{\textit{OOD (Deceptive Delight)}} \\[2pt]
57	9-class (ours) & \textbf{1.000} & .990 & .990 & \textbf{1.000} & \textbf{1.000} \\
58	MLM-only & \textbf{1.000} & \textbf{1.000} & \textbf{1.000} & \textbf{1.000} & \textbf{1.000} \\
59	Vanilla DeBERTa & .575 & .438 & .360 & .311 & .284 \\
60	TF-IDF LR & -- & -- & -- & -- & .944 \\
61	\midrule
62	\multicolumn{6}{l}{\textit{OOD (FITD)}} \\[2pt]
63	9-class (ours) & \textbf{1.000} & \textbf{1.000} & -- & -- & \textbf{1.000} \\
64	Vanilla DeBERTa & .990 & \textbf{1.000} & -- & -- & \textbf{1.000} \\
65	TF-IDF LR & \textbf{1.000} & -- & -- & -- & \textbf{1.000} \\
66	\bottomrule
67	\end{tabular}
68	\end{table*}
69	
70	\begin{figure}[t]
71	\centering
72	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/paper/fig2_cross_attack_bar.pdf}
73	\caption{Cross-attack generalization at full conversation length. Vanilla DeBERTa collapses on unseen Deceptive Delight (F1 = 0.284) while both the 9-class model and the MLM-only variant achieve F1 = 1.000. TF-IDF LR reaches 0.944 on DD.}
74	\label{fig:cross_attack}
75	\end{figure}
76	
77	Table~\ref{tab:main_results} presents F1 scores across three test conditions.
78	The central finding is that MLM continued pretraining, which uses no classification labels, matches or exceeds the full 9-class model on OOD Deceptive Delight at every prefix length.
79	
80	\paragraph{IID performance.}
81	All methods achieve near-perfect F1 on in-distribution Crescendo data.
82	TF-IDF LR and the 9-class model both reach F1 = 1.000 at every prefix length.
83	MLM is slightly below at early prefixes (F1 = 0.960 at $K$=1) but reaches 1.000 by $K$=5, and vanilla DeBERTa ranges from 0.968 to 1.000.
84	The ceiling effect reflects inherent lexical separability between jailbreak and benign conversations: even bag-of-words features suffice when the test distribution matches training.
85	
86	\paragraph{OOD: Deceptive Delight.}
87	The DD evaluation reveals the core result.
88	Vanilla DeBERTa collapses to F1 = 0.284 on full DD conversations despite reaching 1.000 on IID data.
89	The degradation worsens as more turns are observed (F1 drops from 0.575 at $K$=1 to 0.284 at Full), consistent with the finding that multi-turn attack turns are designed to remain in a ``benign'' embedding region~\citep{bullwinkel2025repeng}.
90	The 9-class model maintains F1 $\geq$ 0.990 at every prefix length.
91	The surprise: the MLM variant achieves F1 = 1.000 at every prefix length on DD, matching or slightly exceeding the 9-class model without any classification supervision.
92	This result establishes that domain exposure to jailbreak conversation text is sufficient for cross-attack generalization; the classification objective contributes nothing beyond what continued pretraining provides.
93	TF-IDF LR achieves F1 = 0.944 on full DD conversations, confirming substantial lexical separability even across attack families.
94	
95	\paragraph{OOD: FITD.}
96	FITD conversations are easier to detect across all methods.
97	The 9-class model and vanilla DeBERTa both reach F1 $\geq$ 0.990 at all evaluated prefixes, and TF-IDF LR achieves 1.000 on $K$=1 and full conversations.
98	The limited differentiation on FITD suggests this attack family shares more surface-level features with the Crescendo training data than DD does.
99	
100	%----------------------------------------------------------------------
101	\subsection{Decomposing the Detection Mechanism}
102	\label{sec:decomposition}
103	
104	\begin{table}[t]
105	\centering
106	\small
107	\caption{Evidence hierarchy on OOD Deceptive Delight. All variants share the same Stage-2 pipeline (BiGRU + MLP). Jailbreak-domain continued training (any objective) achieves DD-Full F1 $\geq$ 0.990. Generic continued pretraining (Wikipedia MLM, F1 = 0.742$^\dagger$) provides partial but stable improvement over vanilla (0.284). Misaligned supervision (topic classification, F1 = 0.541$\pm$0.207) yields unstable generalization, with worst seed (0.252) below vanilla. $^\dagger$Single seed; multi-seed pending.}
108	\label{tab:evidence_hierarchy}
109	\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}
110	\toprule
111	\textbf{Variant} & DD-Full & DD-$K$=2 & IID-Full \\
112	\midrule
113	9-class (full model) & \textbf{1.000} & .990 & 1.000 \\
114	MLM-only (no labels) & \textbf{1.000} & \textbf{1.000} & 1.000 \\
115	Binary (2-class) & .990 & .981 & 1.000 \\
116	Scrambled labels & .990 & .981 & 1.000 \\
117	Mean pooling & \textbf{1.000} & \textbf{1.000} & 1.000 \\
118	\midrule
119	Topic classif.\ (5-class) & .541$\pm$.207 & -- & -- \\
120	Wikipedia MLM$^\dagger$ & .742 & .815 & .987 \\
121	Vanilla DeBERTa & .284 & .438 & 1.000 \\
122	\bottomrule
123	\end{tabular}
124	\end{table}
125	
126	We designed seven variants to systematically decompose what enables cross-attack generalization.
127	Table~\ref{tab:evidence_hierarchy} reports DD and IID F1 for each variant.
128	
129	\paragraph{Classification supervision is non-essential.}
130	The MLM variant achieves DD-Full F1 = 1.000 without any classification labels.
131	This is the definitive test: if unsupervised continued pretraining on the training conversations suffices for OOD generalization, then the classification objective (whether 9-class, binary, or scrambled) cannot be the mechanism.
132	The evidence hierarchy is clear:
133	\begin{quote}
134	\small
135	vanilla (0.28) $<$ topic (0.54{\pm}0.21) $<$ wiki\text{-}MLM (0.74) $\ll$ jb\text{-}MLM (1.00) $=$ scrambled (0.99) $=$ binary (0.99) $=$ 9\text{-}class (1.00)
136	\end{quote}
137	Every variant that includes jailbreak-domain continued training achieves DD-Full F1 $\geq$ 0.990.
138	The 70+ percentage point gap lies entirely between vanilla and any form of jailbreak-domain continued training, not between MLM and classification variants.
139	
140	\paragraph{Label quality and granularity are non-factors.}
141	Replacing the 9-class taxonomy with randomly scrambled labels reduces DD-Full F1 from 1.000 to 0.990, a gap of 1 percentage point.
142	Collapsing to binary yields the same 0.990.
143	Both remain far above vanilla (F1 = 0.284), a gap exceeding 70 percentage points.
144	These results confirm the domain-exposure interpretation~\citep{gururangan2020dontstop}: the encoder learns useful features from exposure to persuasion-domain text during continued training, regardless of whether the supervision signal is correct, coarse, or random.
145	
146	\paragraph{Misaligned supervision destabilizes generalization.}
147	The topic classification control replaces the persuasion strategy head with a 5-class topic classifier on the same training text, with no intent head.
148	Across three seeds (42, 123, 456), this variant yields DD-Full F1 = 0.541$\pm$0.207 (individual seeds: 0.727, 0.252, 0.644).
149	The high variance is itself informative: seed 123 achieves F1 = 0.252, \emph{below} the vanilla baseline (0.284), meaning misaligned supervision can leave the encoder worse off than no adaptation at all.
150	Random labels ($\approx$ no labels at 0.990) and no labels (MLM at 1.000) both substantially and stably outperform meaningful but irrelevant labels (topic at 0.541$\pm$0.207).
151	The interpretation is that a misaligned classification objective forces the encoder to allocate capacity to features irrelevant to jailbreak detection, not merely reducing the benefit of domain exposure but actively destabilizing the learned representations.
152	Persuasion classification is ``task-compatible'' (it does not hurt relative to MLM) but not additive: the persuasion labels neither help nor hinder the domain-exposure mechanism.
153	
154	\paragraph{Temporal modeling is unnecessary.}
155	Replacing BiGRU with orderless mean pooling yields DD-Full F1 = 1.000, matching the BiGRU default (Table~\ref{tab:evidence_hierarchy}).
156	The detection signal resides in individual turn representations rather than their sequential arrangement.
157	BiGRU is the default aggregator in all other experiments.
158	
159	\paragraph{Loss weighting is insensitive.}
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01K9JcnfdmfZNSuUREyvS5Tp`</summary>

```text
159	\paragraph{Loss weighting is insensitive.}
160	We evaluated $\alpha \in \{0, 0.3, 0.5, 2.0\}$ for the multi-task loss weight.
161	All values achieve IID-Full F1 = 1.000.
162	The $\alpha=0$ variant (persuasion strategy classification only, no intent head) confirms that the persuasion task alone suffices for in-distribution detection.
163	
164	\paragraph{Domain specificity is required.}
165	A Wikipedia MLM control applies continued pretraining on out-of-domain text with matched token count ($\sim$169K tokens).
166	This variant achieves DD-Full F1 = 0.742 (single seed; multi-seed pending), far below jailbreak-domain MLM (1.000).
167	Generic continued pretraining provides substantial improvement over vanilla (+46 percentage points) but fails to bridge the full gap.
168	Wikipedia MLM (0.742) is notably more stable than misaligned topic classification (0.541$\pm$0.207): wrong domain with a compatible objective produces consistent partial improvement, whereas correct domain with a misaligned objective yields erratic results.
169	Only correct domain with a compatible or absent task objective reaches F1 $\geq$ 0.99.
170	This confirms that the mechanism is not continued pretraining in general but exposure to the specific distributional patterns of jailbreak conversation text, and that misaligned supervision actively interferes with this mechanism.
171	
172	%----------------------------------------------------------------------
173	\subsection{Robustness Under Adversarial Perturbation}
174	\label{sec:perturbation}
175	
176	\begin{table}[t]
177	\centering
178	\small
179	\caption{Robustness under adversarial paraphrasing (IID test set, full conversations). Perturbed set preserves semantics (BERTScore 0.92) while altering surface forms (lexical overlap 0.20). $\Delta$: degradation in percentage points.}
180	\label{tab:perturbation}
181	\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}
182	\toprule
183	\textbf{Method} & Original & Perturbed & $\Delta$ (pp) \\
184	\midrule
185	9-class (ours) & \textbf{1.000} & \textbf{1.000} & \textbf{0.0} \\
186	MLM-only & 1.000 & .974 & $-$2.6 \\
187	TF-IDF LR & 1.000 & .987 & $-$1.3 \\
188	Vanilla DeBERTa & 1.000 & .882 & $-$11.8 \\
189	\bottomrule
190	\end{tabular}
191	\end{table}
192	
193	Table~\ref{tab:perturbation} reports F1 on the original and perturbed IID test sets.
194	The 9-class model achieves F1 = 1.000 on both, with zero degradation.
195	The MLM-only variant drops from 1.000 to 0.974 (2.6 percentage points), and vanilla DeBERTa drops from 1.000 to 0.882 (11.8 percentage points).
196	TF-IDF LR shows a 1.3 percentage point decline.
197	
198	This result provides directional evidence that classification supervision contributes robustness beyond domain exposure alone.
199	Domain-adaptive continued training (MLM) improves substantially over vanilla DeBERTa (2.6 vs.\ 11.8 percentage points of degradation), and the 9-class model eliminates the remaining gap.
200	However, two caveats apply.
201	First, the test set contains only 76 samples, so the 2.6 percentage point difference between MLM and 9-class corresponds to approximately two additional misclassifications; larger-scale evaluation is needed to confirm this gap.
202	Second, the 9-class model combines persuasion strategy and intent classification heads; we have not tested perturbation robustness for the scrambled, binary, or topic variants, so we cannot attribute the robustness gain specifically to persuasion labels rather than to multi-task classification more broadly.
203	With these caveats, the preliminary evidence suggests that domain exposure alone suffices for cross-attack detection (Section~\ref{sec:decomposition}), while classification supervision may provide additional perturbation robustness.
204	
205	%----------------------------------------------------------------------
206	\subsection{LLM-as-Judge Baseline}
207	\label{sec:llm_judge}
208	
```

model\"。\n\n**建议**: Classification supervision 不仅增加 perturbation robustness，还显著增加 seed stability。这实际上**强化**了 9-class model 的价值论述——D023 的新叙事方向是正确的。\n\n#### [severity: critical] F3: Perturbation table 缺失已完成的 binary/topic/wiki MLM 数据\n\nexperiments.tex L202 仍写 \"we have not tested perturbation robustness for the scrambled, binary, or topic variants\"。但以下数据已完成：\n\n完整 perturbation 表 (delta at Full)：\n| Variant | Delta Full |\n|---|---|\n| 9-class | **0.000** |\n| Binary | **0.000** |\n| TF-IDF | -0.013 |\n| JB MLM | -0.026 |\n| Topic | -0.040 |\n| Vanilla | -0.118 |\n| Wiki MLM | -0.121 |\n| Scrambled | INVALID (数据不匹配) |\n\nBinary delta=0.000 at Full 是重要发现——可以用来回答\"robustness gain 是否来自 persuasion labels 还是 multi-task classification generally\"：binary (2-class) 也零退化，说明 multi-task classification（无论 granularity）都添加 robustness。\n\n#### [severity: warning] F4: Binary per-K perturbation 揭示 9-class fine-grained labels 的额外价值\n\n| K | 9-class Δ | Binary Δ | JB MLM Δ |\n|---|---|---|---|\n| K=1 | -0.026 | **-0.092** | -0.092 |\n| K=2 | 0.000 | -0.026 | -0.040 |\n| K=3 | 0.000 | 0.000 | -0.040 |\n| Full | 0.000 | 0.000 | -0.026 |\n\nK=1 时 binary 和 MLM 退化幅度相同(-0.092)，远高于 9-class(-0.026)。**Fine-grained 9-class labels 对 early-turn perturbation robustness 有独特贡献**，粗粒度 binary 不够。这支持保留 9-class taxonomy 的理由。\n\n注意：论文 abstract L9 \"zero degradation\" 仅在 Full 正确；K=1 时 9-class 也有 -0.026 退化，应限定表述。\n\n#### [severity: warning] F5: Domain specificity 在 K=1-5 成立，Full 不成立\n\n| K | JB MLM | Wiki MLM | Gap |\n|---|--------|----------|-----|\n| K=1 | 0.973±0.019 | 0.913±0.073 | +6pp |\n| K=2 | 0.993±0.009 | 0.911±0.073 | +8pp |\n| K=3 | 0.997±0.005 | 0.937±0.062 | +6pp |\n| K=5 | 0.997±0.005 | 0.923±0.082 | +7pp |\n| Full | 0.914±0.115 | 0.904±0.115 | **+1pp** |\n\nDomain specificity 在 K=1-5 一致存在（6-8pp gap + JB MLM variance 远低于 wiki MLM）。Full-turn gap 仅 1pp，统计不可区分。论文如果 focus 在 K=1-5，domain specificity claim 是可辩护的。\n\n#### [severity: warning] F6: Scrambled perturbation 确认无效（重复 Report #62）\n\nClean/perturbed F1 均为 0.333（全部预测 benign），因 retrain 使用了 final_v2 训练数据（336 samples）而非 plan_002_splits。在论文 perturbation table 中应标为 N/A 或诚实解释。\n\n#### [severity: info] F7: D023 叙事方向正确\n\nD023 将叙事从\"domain exposure is the mechanism\"调整为\"any continued pretraining helps, but classification supervision provides stability, robustness, and interpretability\"。Multi-seed 数据完全支持这个新方向。关键论据链：\n- Detection: 9-class 0.997±0.005 >> JB MLM 0.914±0.115（稳定性）\n- Perturbation: 9-class delta=0.000 = binary delta=0.000 > MLM delta=-0.026 > vanilla delta=-0.118\n- Domain specificity: 成立于 K=1-5（6-8pp gap），不成立于 Full\n\n### 建议\n\n1. **论文必须立即反映 D023**：6 处旧数据全部更新，evidence hierarchy 重写\n2. **Table 2 加 multi-seed 列**：至少 JB MLM 和 wiki MLM 应报告 3-seed mean±std\n3. **Table 3 (perturbation) 扩充**：加入 binary (0.000), topic (-0.040), wiki MLM (-0.121)\n4. **删除 experiments.tex L202 \"未测试\" 声明**：binary/topic/wiki MLM perturbation 均已完成\n5. **Domain specificity claim 重新定位为 K=1-5 phenomenon**\n6. **新 contribution point**: 9-class seed stability advantage (0.997±0.005 vs 0.914±0.115)",

```json
}
```

```text
9-class seed 123 DD OOD results:
- Full: 1.000
- K=1: 1.000
- K=2: 0.990
- K=3: 0.990
- K=5: 1.000

This matches what I had from plan_002_seed456_dd_ood.json earlier. Let me now compile the complete 9-class multi-seed DD OOD data:

- Seed 42: Full=1.000, K=1=1.000, K=2=0.990, K=3=0.990, K=5=1.000 (from original plan_002)


- Seed 123: Full=1.000, K=1=1.000, K=2=0.990, K=3=0.990, K=5=1.000
- Seed 456: Full=0.990, K=1=0.971, K=2=0.962, K=3=0.935, K=5=0.990

Now I'm calculating the means and standard deviations across these three seeds. The full model averages to about 0.997 with minimal variance, K=1 comes in around 0.991, and K=2 sits at 0.981, while K=3 shows more variation at 0.938.

The 9-class model is remarkably stable across all seeds and K levels, with no performance collapse even at K=3. This is notably better than the JB MLM baseline which hit 0.751 and the wiki MLM at 0.742. There's a discrepancy with D023's reported value of 1.000±0.000 for Persuasion—my calculation shows 0.997±0.005 because seed 456 actually achieved 0.990 rather than a perfect score.

The IID sanity check confirms the model's robustness: seed 123 achieves perfect 1.000 across all conditions, matching seed 42's Full performance. Only seed 456 dips slightly to 0.990, which is still near-perfect. This validates the findings from Report #63, so I'll send a brief confirmation noting that the seed 123 results reinforce the stability advantage of the 9-class approach.
```

```text
Wait - this is slightly different from what I reported. The ROADMAP says 9-class is 1.00±0.005 but my calculation showed 0.997±0.005. 

The difference is just rounding, but I should be precise. I'm also noticing TF-IDF (0.94) is positioned between wiki-MLM and scrambled—that's its single-seed DD Full F1 score of 0.944. What's striking is that TF-IDF's single-seed result outperforms both wiki-MLM and JB-MLM's multi-seed means, which raises questions about whether the multi-seed approach is actually providing the expected benefit.

Looking at the single-seed comparisons more carefully: JB-MLM hits 1.0 with seed 42, TF-IDF is at 0.944, and wiki-MLM drops to 0.742. But when I compare multi-seed means, JB-MLM averages 0.914 while TF-IDF stays at 0.944 as a single measurement. The key insight is that TF-IDF uses simple TF-IDF plus logistic regression without a GRU stage, so it doesn't suffer from the seed variance that affects the MLM approaches. That 0.944 is likely quite stable, which means TF-IDF might actually outperform JB-MLM's multi-seed average—and that's problematic for the narrative I'm building.

The paper does acknowledge this somewhat by emphasizing that DisPeD's real advantage lies in robustness under perturbation and interpretability rather than raw F1 scores. But there's a consistency issue in how the results are presented: the hierarchy mixes single-seed measurements with multi-seed means, so wiki-MLM and JB-MLM are reported as multi-seed averages while TF-IDF, scrambled, and binary are single-seed values, making direct comparison misleading.

The crucial insight is that scrambled and binary both use classification supervision like 9-class does, so they should show similar stability across multiple seeds if tested that way. Without multi-seed evaluation for scrambled and binary, I can't determine whether the stability comes from having classification supervision in general or specifically from having correct labels like 9-class does.

Looking at the roadmap now, I've completed all 40 experiments and have a solid evidence hierarchy with multi-seed data. The four-layer value structure is coherent, and I have a comfortable 7-day buffer before the deadline with full team availability. The main challenge ahead is compressing the paper from 14 pages down to 8 while preserving the narrative—that's a significant cut that will require careful prioritization of content.

There are some methodological inconsistencies I need to address: the evidence hierarchy is mixing single-seed and multi-seed results, which makes direct comparisons problematic. TF-IDF shows 0.94 on single-seed while JB MLM has 0.91±0.12 with multi-seed, so I need to ensure I'm comparing apples to apples. The scrambled retrain is still flagged as invalid due to data mismatch, and I should either fix this with a proper retrain or explicitly acknowledge the limitation in the paper. I'm also missing multi-seed data for binary and scrambled DD OOD—currently relying on single-seed 0.99 values, which makes it hard to claim they're definitively more stable than JB MLM without that validation.

For D024, I've confirmed the 9-class DD OOD result of 0.997±0.005 is correct, though D023's claimed 1.000±0.000 doesn't match. The architecture figure needs to display all 7 variants, which is a substantial design challenge. I'm going to prepare a focused report on these ROADMAP issues.
```
